Semiparametric Approach to Estimation of Marginal and Quantile Effects
Seong-ho Lee, Yanyuan Ma, Elvezio Ronchetti

Abstract
We consider a semiparametric generalized linear model and study estimation of both marginal and quantile effects in this model. We propose an approximate maximum likelihood estimator, and rigorously establish the consistency, the asymptotic normality, and the semiparametric efficiency of our method in both the marginal effect and the quantile effect estimation. Simulation studies are conducted to illustrate the finite sample performance, and we apply the new tool to analyze a Swiss non-labor income data and discover a new interesting predictor.



Keywords:
generalized linear model,
marginal effect, quantile effect, misspecification, robustness, semiparametric efficiency.

1
Introduction

Generalized linear models (GLMs)
(
McCullagh & Nelder 1989
)
are arguably the most
frequently used models in statistical research and application. With response
variable
Y\in\mathbb{R}
and covariates
{\bf X}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, GLMs
have the familiar form

\displaystyle f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y,{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}},\phi)=\exp\left\{\frac{y{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}-b({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})}{a(\phi)}+c(y,\phi)\right\},

(1)

where
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is an unknown regression coefficient vector, and
\phi
is
typically an unknown scalar parameter, if it is present. Here,
the functions
a(\cdot),b(\cdot)
, and
c(\cdot)
have pre-specified
forms, and various choices of these functions lead to different GLMs
extensively studied in the statistical literature.

While a large body of the classic literature is mainly concerned with the
estimation and the inference of the model parameters
{\bm{\beta}}
and
\phi
in
(
1
), an equally important target of research is to
estimate marginal effect, say
{\bm{\xi}}
, defined as

\displaystyle{\bm{\xi}}\equiv E\left\{\frac{\partial E(Y\mid{\bf X})}{\partial{\bf X}}\right\}.

(2)

The marginal effect is a quantity of important meaning in several fields,
in particular econometrics, and is used to
capture the average rate of change of the regression mean with respect
to the covariates
(
Greene 2000
)
.

A complementary, less known but important quantity is
quantile effect, defined as

\displaystyle{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}\equiv E\left\{\frac{\partial Q_{\tau}(Y\mid{\bf X})}{\partial{\bf X}}\right\}

(3)

for a continuous response variable
Y
,
where
\tau\in(0,1)
is the quantile level and

Q_{\tau}(Y\mid{\bf x})
is the
\tau
th conditional quantile of
Y
given

{\bf x}
. Here, we
restrict our attention to the continuous response case only
because the definition of conditional quantile in the discrete case
is
not universally agreed upon in the literature
(
Parzen 2004
)
.
The quantile effect measures
the average rate of change in the conditional quantiles,
and
can be equivalently written as

E\{{\bm{\beta}}Q_{\tau}^{\prime}(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}
,
where
Q_{\tau}^{\prime}(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})
is the derivative of

Q_{\tau}(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})
with respect to
{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}
.
Obviously, our consideration of quantile effect
is a natural consequence of considering
quantile regression, which is a key statistical tool widely used
as an alternative to the traditional mean regression models.
Instead of modeling the conditional mean,
the quantile regression estimates the relationship
between the covariates and the response through the conditional quantile

Q_{\tau}(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})
, and considering its corresponding
rate of change directly leads to the quantile effect defined in
(
3
). Comparing (
2
) and
(
3
), it is clear that
{\bm{\xi}}
and
{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
are in fact
marginal mean effect and marginal quantile effect respectively, while
we shorten the names to marginal effect and quantile
effect.

Marginal effect has direct interpretation as
measures of risk.
For instance, in logistic regression, especially in the medical
literature, it is customary
to misinterpret odds ratios as measures of risk. Instead, marginal
and quantile
effects represent changes in the probability of the occurrence of a binary event
with respect to given risk factors and have direct interpretation
on the
probability scale. This point has been stressed in the medical
literature, e.g., by

Norton et al. 2019
. Marginal effect also captures how
strongly the mean treatment outcome relies on different covariates on
average. This further leads to the understanding on how average treatment
effect is affected by covariates. Treatment effect is directly linked
to causal inference which is currently one of the trending topics in
Statistics, as further confirmed by the 2021 Nobel Prize in Economics
based on the seminal paper by
Imbens & Angrist 1994
.
In fact,
estimation of marginal effect has been studied by many earlier works
in the statistics literature, where the name “average mean
derivatives estimation” was adopted. For example,

Härdle & Stoker 1989
systematically studied the estimation
problem,
and
Newey & Stoker 1993
studied semiparametric efficiency
properties
of weighted average mean derivative estimators.
Likewise, quantile effect, formerly termed as
average quantile derivatives,
has also been proposed and studied as an alternative to the marginal
effect by
Chaudhuri et al. 1997
.
Following these earlier works,
different approaches to estimating marginal effect under various
settings have been proposed, see,
e.g.,
Hristache et al. 2001
,
Cattaneo et al. 2010
, and
Cattaneo et al. 2013
.
The estimation of marginal effect is also quite widespread in medical
economics concerning health outcomes.
For instance, a two-year study performed at the University of
Chicago focuses on marginal effect on expenditures and length of stays
in hospitals, rather than
on parameter estimation of GLM; see
Basu & Rathouz 2005
. For the same study,

Manning et al. 2005
estimate marginal effect
on inpatient
expenditures
using a parametric family of distributions, a three parameter generalized Gamma
distribution, which is an extension of the GLMs.
Marginal effect is also frequently the target of study in
economics. Parametric estimation
of marginal effect in microeconometrics
concerning the female labor force participation for 872 women from
Switzerland can be found in
Gerfin 1996
and
Kleiber & Zeileis 2008
.
We will use this example as a benchmark to compare our nonparametric estimation
in Section
5
.
In a GLM, the marginal effect happens to be
identical to the regression coefficient
{\bm{\beta}}
multiplied by
average conditional variance, i.e.

{\bm{\xi}}={\bm{\beta}}E\left\{\hbox{var}(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\right\}
.
Similarly for the quantile effect, we have

{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}={\bm{\beta}}E\left\{Q_{\tau}^{\prime}(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\right\}
.
This allows
for their straightforward estimation.
Standard statistical packages provide direct estimation of marginal effect under GLMs,
including the
margins
command in Stata and the
package
mfx
in R
(
Fernihough 2019
)
.

However, despite its central role in the statistical literature and its wide
application, GLM has its restrictions. Indeed,
GLM prespecifies the three functions
a(\cdot),b(\cdot)
, and

c(\cdot)
hence is purely parametric, therefore the model is susceptible to
model misspecification. In addition, to facilitate
computation, the choices of the three functions are often out of
convenience, which further increases the chance of mis-modeling.
To take into account the possibly large impact of deviation from the
distributional assumptions on the
parameter estimates and on the corresponding inference based on GLMs,
a broad stream of the literature in the past decades has focused on robust methods
for the estimation of the parameters;
see e.g.

Stefanski et al. 1986
,
Künsch et al. 1989
, and
Cantoni & Ronchetti 2001
for a general class of optimally bounded influence estimators and tests for the parameters
of GLMs. The corresponding formulas for the estimation of
marginal and quantile effects remain the same
as the parametric case, but with the robust estimates of the parameters.

Here we go one step further in relaxing
the parametric form of GLMs and consider a
semiparametric generalized linear model (sGLM), where we assume

\displaystyle f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y,{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}},c)=\frac{\exp\left\{y{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}+c(y)\right\}}{\int\exp\left\{y{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}+c(y)\right\}d\mu(y)},

(4)

and leave
c(\cdot)
unspecified.
Here and in the following text,
\mu(\cdot)
denotes the
Lebesgue measure for a continuous
variable
y
and the counting measure for a discrete
y
.
For identifiability, we fix
c(0)=0
and for convenience,
we assume each component of
{\bf x}
to be centered so

E({\bf X})={\bf 0}
. Our focus is to estimate marginal and quantile effects
of (
4
), i.e.
{\bm{\xi}}
defined in (
2
) and

{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
in (
3
).

Model (
4
) is not entirely new, and has been proposed and
studied in

Luo & Tsai 2012
and
Huang & Rathouz 2012

as a semiparametric proportional likelihood
ratio model.

Ning et al. 2017
studied high dimensional issue in
(
4
), while
Lin et al. 2021
studied the estimation of the
parameter
{\bm{\beta}}
in (
4
).
Different from these existing
works, our main focus is not in the model parameter
{\bm{\beta}}
, but in the
marginal effect
{\bm{\xi}}
and the quantile effect
{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
.

In this context, we develop an approximate
likelihood
procedure to estimate both the model parameters
{\bm{\beta}}
and
c(\cdot)

in (
4
), which allows us to
subsequently estimate the marginal effect
{\bm{\xi}}
and the quantile
effect
{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
. We show that despite of
the apparent difference of the estimation variability results from the
theoretical efficiency bounds, our
estimators are in fact semiparametricly efficient for both
{\bm{\xi}}
and

{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
.
We point out that our method differs from
Lin et al. 2021

primarily in how to estimate the nonparametric component
c(y)
,
which in turn has significant influence on estimation of the model parameters
and functionals. Due to the
special structure of model (
4
), kernel estimation
for
c(y)

is difficult to implement without giving up the model information
and resorting to model-free estimation
such as the Nadaraya-Watson estimator,
hence the estimation of
c(y)
in
Lin et al. 2021
is not ideal.
This leads to efficiency loss of the
{\bm{\beta}}
estimation in finite samples,
and greatly affects the efficiency of the
{\bm{\xi}}
and
{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
estimation.
The efficiency gain from our method is demonstrated by simulation
experiments
in Section
4
.

Specifically, our main contributions to the literature
are the following.
First, we define and consider estimating
marginal effect and quantile effect in a semiparametric model, which
have been studied by many earlier works in Statistics under various
settings and
are receiving more attention due to the recent
interest in mean treatment effect or mean outcome estimation.
Second, we
discover that in this specific problem, kernel estimation
may not be a good approach to estimate the function
c(y)
while
B-spline based estimation fits naturally and has a
clear advantage.
This
is not usually the case in many semiparametric estimation problems.
Third, we provide a clear and transparent estimation procedure, which is
computationally simple and enjoys the advantage of convex
optimization.
Fourth, we establish the optimality of the resulting marginal and quantile
effect estimators as functionals of the model parameters. This
optimality is not a straightforward result to
obtain. It requires keen statistical insight to link two seemingly unrelevant
results and the right mathematical tools to achieve it.

The rest of the article is organized as the following. In Section

2
, we provide the main methodological
results of our work and describe the estimation procedures for
both
{\bm{\xi}}
and
{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
.
In Section
3
,
we establish the asymptotic properties of
our estimators, compare them to the theoretical efficiency bounds, and
show that they in fact reach the efficiency bounds.
Simulation studies are conducted in Section
4
to
illustrate the finite sample performance of our method in comparison
with existing methods. An interesting dataset concerning
Swiss non-labor income is analyzed in Section
5
,
where our nonparametric analysis reveals new insights concerning model
choice, marginal effect, and significant covariates that would remain hidden
if using a strict parametric approach.
We conclude our paper with a discussion in Section
6
. All
the technical details are relegated to the supplementary material.

2
Methodology

2.1
Efficiency Bound of Marginal Effect Estimation

Before engaging ourselves in the estimation and inference of the
marginal effect
{\bm{\xi}}
associated with the model (
4
), we
would like first to understand the limit of our endeavor by
establishing the efficiency bound of the
{\bm{\xi}}
estimation.
Let
v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})\equiv E[\{Y-E(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})\}^{2}\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}]
. The derivation
detailed in Section
S.1
leads to
the efficient influence function as

\displaystyle\bm{\phi}_{\rm eff}(y,{\bf x})={\bm{\beta}}v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})-{\bm{\beta}}E\{v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}+{\bm{\beta}}y^{2}+{\bf M}{\bf x}y+{\bf a}(y)-E\{{\bm{\beta}}Y^{2}+{\bf M}{\bf x}Y+{\bf a}(Y)\mid{\bf x}\},

where
{\bf a}(y)
satisfies

\displaystyle E[E\{{\bf a}(Y)\mid{\bf X}\}\mid y]-{\bf a}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2{\bm{\beta}}E[yE(Y\mid{\bf X})-E\{YE(Y\mid{\bf X})\mid{\bf X}\}\mid y]+{\bf M}E[{\bf X}\{y-E(Y\mid{\bf X})\}\mid y].

and

\displaystyle{\bf M}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(E\{v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}{\bf I}-E\left[2{\bm{\beta}}{\bf X}^{\rm T}Yv({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})+{\bf a}(Y)\{Y-E(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}{\bf X}^{\rm T}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\times\left[E\{{\bf X}{\bf X}^{\rm T}v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}\right]^{-1},

Obviously, the variance of the efficient influence function,
i.e.
\hbox{var}\{\bm{\phi}_{\rm eff}(Y,{\bf X})\}
, is the efficiency bound in
estimating
{\bm{\xi}}
.

2.2
Efficiency Bound of Quantile Effect Estimation

In Section
S.2
, we further derive the efficient influence function of
{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}

under (
4
).
Now for notational brevity, let

q(\nu)\equiv Q_{\tau}(Y\mid\nu)
,
q^{\prime}(\nu)\equiv Q^{\prime}_{\tau}(Y\mid\nu)
,

q^{\prime\prime}(\nu)\equiv Q^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}(Y\mid\nu)
,

\epsilon(\nu)\equiv\tau-I\{Y<q(\nu)\}
,
and
\epsilon^{\prime}(\nu)\equiv-\delta\{q(\nu)-Y\}q^{\prime}(\nu)
.
The efficient influence function turns out to be

\displaystyle\bm{\phi}_{\rm eff}(y,{\bf x})={\bm{\beta}}q^{\prime}({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})-{\bm{\beta}}E\{q^{\prime}({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}+{\bf M}_{1}{\bf x}y+{\bf a}(y)-E\{{\bf M}_{1}{\bf x}Y+{\bf a}(Y)|{\bf x}\},

where
{\bf a}(y)
satisfies

\displaystyle E[E\{{\bf a}(Y)\mid{\bf X}\}\mid y]-{\bf a}(y)=-{\bm{\beta}}E\{r(y,{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})|y\}+{\bf M}_{1}E[{\bf X}\{y-E(Y|{\bf X})\}|y],

in which

\displaystyle r(Y,\nu)\equiv\frac{\epsilon(\nu)Y+\epsilon^{\prime}(\nu)-\epsilon(\nu)[q(\nu)+q^{\prime}(\nu)\nu+q^{\prime}(\nu)c^{\prime}\{q(\nu)\}]}{f\{q(\nu),\nu\}},

and

\displaystyle{\bf M}_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(E\{q^{\prime}({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}{\bf I}+{\bm{\beta}}E\{{\bf X}^{\rm T}q^{\prime\prime}({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}-E[{\bf a}(Y){\bf X}^{\rm T}\{Y-E(Y|{\bf X})\}])

\displaystyle\times[E\{{\bf X}{\bf X}^{\rm T}v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}]^{-1}.

Similar to the case for
estimating
{\bm{\xi}}
, the variance of the efficient influence function is
the efficiency bound for estimating
{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
.

2.3
Estimation Procedure

We now propose an estimation procedure under the model (
4
).
We first consider the case where the response
Y
is distributed
continuously.
In such case, we approximate
c(\cdot)

of the conditional density
f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y,{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}},c)
by a B-spline
curve
{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bm{\gamma}}
,
where
{\bf B}(\cdot)\equiv\{B_{1}(\cdot),\dots,B_{m}(\cdot)\}^{\rm T}
is a
B-spline basis vector
and
{\bm{\gamma}}\equiv(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{m})^{\rm T}
is an unknown
coefficient of the bases.
Since we assume
c(0)=0
for identifiability,
we ignore the first B-spline basis
B_{1}(\cdot)
which corresponds to
the intercept of the curve,
by letting
\gamma_{1}=0
.
Therefore, we replace
c(y)
in (
4
) by
{\bf B}(y)^{\rm T}{\bm{\gamma}}

with
\gamma_{1}=0
,
and estimate
{\bm{\beta}}
and
{\bm{\gamma}}
through maximizing the approximate
loglikelihood

\displaystyle l({\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[y_{i}{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}_{i}+{\bf B}(y_{i})^{\rm T}{\bm{\gamma}}-\hbox{log}\int\exp\left\{y{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}_{i}+{\bf B}(y)^{\rm T}{\bm{\gamma}}\right\}d\mu(y)\right].

(5)

Note that the loglikelihood
l({\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}})
is a concave
function, hence the optimizer is unique and can be readily obtained
using off-the-shelf convex optimizers. In our implementation, we
used the built-in R function
optim
for optimization,
yet found the computation fast with
satisfactory performance. For example,
in our real data analysis where the dataset contains
871
observations
and
7
predictors,
it takes less than 10 seconds
to obtain the optimizer within the relative tolerance of
10^{-6}
.

Once we obtain
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
and
\widehat{c}(\cdot)\equiv{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}
,
we can easily estimate the marginal effect
{\bm{\xi}}
using (
2
) through

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\xi}}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\widehat{E}\left\{\widehat{\hbox{var}}(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\,n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\int y^{2}f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y,{\bf x}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\widehat{c})d\mu(y)-\left\{\int yf_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y,{\bf x}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\widehat{c})d\mu(y)\right\}^{2}\right].

Similarly, we estimate the quantile effect
{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
using (
3
) through

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\widehat{E}\left\{\widehat{Q}^{\prime}_{\tau}(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\,n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\tau\int_{0}^{1}yf_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y,{\bf x}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\widehat{c})d\mu(y)-\int_{0}^{q_{i}}yf_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y,{\bf x}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\widehat{c})d\mu(y)}{f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(q_{i},{\bf x}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\widehat{c})}\bigg|_{q_{i}=\widehat{Q}_{\tau}(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}_{i})},

where
\widehat{Q}_{\tau}(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}_{i}),i=1,\dots,n
,
the estimated conditional
\tau
th quantiles of
Y
given
{\bf x}_{i}
,
are obtained by solving for
q_{i}
from

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{q_{i}}f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y,{\bf x}_{i},\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\widehat{c})d\mu(y)=\tau.

When the response
Y
is a categorical variable taking values
\{0,...,m\}
,
then the goal is to estimate
{\bm{\beta}}
and
c(0),\dots,c(m)
. In this
case, (
4
) is a purely parametric model and we can proceed
with maximum likelihood estimation (MLE). An alternative way of
viewing this is that we replace
c(\cdot)
by
{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bm{\gamma}}

where
{\bf B}(\cdot)\equiv\{I(\cdot=0),\dots,I(\cdot=m)\}^{\rm T}
with
\gamma_{1}=0
,
and maximize the approximate loglikelihood given in (
5
)
with respect to
{\bm{\beta}}
and
{\bm{\gamma}}
.
Having obtained
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
and
\widehat{c}(\cdot)
, we use

\displaystyle\hbox{pr}(Y=y|{\bf x};{\bm{\beta}},c)=\frac{\exp\{y{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}+c(y)\}}{\sum_{y=0}^{m}\exp\{y{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}+c(y)\}},

y=0,\dots,m
to estimate the marginal effect
{\bm{\xi}}
by

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\xi}}\equiv\widehat{\bm{\beta}}\,n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\sum_{y=0}^{m}y^{2}\,\hbox{pr}(Y=y|{\bf x}_{i};\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\widehat{c})-\left\{\sum_{y=0}^{m}y\,\hbox{pr}(Y=y|{\bf x}_{i};\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\widehat{c})\right\}^{2}\right].

Because there is no generally accepted unique way of defining quantiles
for discrete data, we do not further study the quantile effect
estimation in this case.

3
Theoretical Properties

We now establish the theoretical properties of our proposed estimators
for both the marginal effect and the quantile effect in
(
4
).

3.1
Continuous Response

First we analyze the properties of our estimators
under the continuous response case. We simplify

d\mu(y)
as
dy
below.
To set notation, let

\displaystyle f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}^{*}(y,{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}})

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{\exp\{y{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}+{\bf B}(y)^{\rm T}{\bm{\gamma}}\}}{\int\exp\{y{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}+{\bf B}(y)^{\rm T}{\bm{\gamma}}\}dy},

\displaystyle E^{*}\left\{{\bf g}(Y)|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}}\right\}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int{\bf g}(y)f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}^{*}(y,{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}})dy,

\displaystyle\hbox{var}^{*}\left(Y|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}}\right)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle E^{*}(Y^{2}|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}})-\left\{E^{*}(Y|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}})\right\}^{2},

\displaystyle\hbox{cov}^{*}\left\{{\bf g}(Y),{\bf h}(Y)|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}}\right\}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle E^{*}\left\{{\bf g}(Y){\bf h}(Y)^{\rm T}|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}}\right\}

\displaystyle-E^{*}\left\{{\bf g}(Y)|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}}\right\}E^{*}\left\{{\bf h}(Y)^{\rm T}|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}}\right\},

where we use
E^{*}
instead of
E
to emphasize that the expectation is
computed under the approximate model, which has the same form as
(
4
), but with
c(\cdot)
replaced by
{\bf B}^{\rm T}(\cdot){\bm{\gamma}}
.

For real numbers
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
,

a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
denotes
a_{n}=O(b_{n})
and
b_{n}=O(a_{n})
simultaneously.
Similarly, for random variables
A_{n}
and
B_{n}
,

A_{n}\asymp_{p}B_{n}
denotes
A_{n}=O_{p}(B_{n})
and
B_{n}=O_{p}(A_{n})
simultaneously.
For a vector
{\bf a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{d})^{\rm T}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
we denote the
l_{p}
-norm of
{\bf a}
as

\|{\bf a}\|_{p}\equiv(|a_{1}|^{p}+\dots+|a_{d}|^{p})^{1/p}
,
1\leq p\leq\infty
.
For a matrix
{\bf A}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times c}
,
we denote the induced
l_{p}
-norm of
{\bf A}
as

\|{\bf A}\|_{p}\equiv\sup_{{\bf u}\in\mathbb{R}^{c},\|{\bf u}\|_{p}=1}\|{\bf A}{\bf u}\|_{p}
,
1\leq p\leq\infty
.
For a function
g(\cdot)
in the
L^{2}
space,
we denote its
L_{p}
-norm as

\lVert g(\cdot)\rVert_{p}\equiv\{\int_{0}^{1}|g(y)|^{p}dy\}^{1/p}
.
We denote the set of the
q
th order smooth functions as

C^{q}([0,1])\equiv\{g:g^{(q)}\in C([0,1])\}
.

To facilitate the theoretical derivation,
we view the estimation procedure described in Section

2.3
alternatively as a profile procedure.
Specifically, treating
{\bm{\beta}}
as a fixed parameter,
estimate
c(\cdot)
by the spline curve
\widehat{c}(\cdot,{\bm{\beta}})\equiv{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}({\bm{\beta}})

via maximizing the approximate loglikelihood

\displaystyle l({\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(y_{i}{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}_{i}+{\bf B}(y_{i})^{\rm T}{\bm{\gamma}}-\hbox{log}\left[\int_{0}^{1}\exp\{y{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}_{i}+{\bf B}(y)^{\rm T}{\bm{\gamma}}\}dy\right]\right)

with respect to
{\bm{\gamma}}
. Then estimate
{\bm{\beta}}
by maximizing

\displaystyle\widehat{l}({\bm{\beta}})\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(y_{i}{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}_{i}+\widehat{c}(y_{i},{\bm{\beta}})-\hbox{log}\left[\int_{0}^{1}\exp\{y{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}_{i}+\widehat{c}(y,{\bm{\beta}})\}dy\right]\right).

We point out that profiling and
performing maximization jointly with respect to
{\bm{\beta}}
and
{\bm{\gamma}}
yield
the same result.

In the following, we first aim at proving the convergence property of

\widehat{c}(y,{\bm{\beta}})
when
{\bm{\beta}}={\bm{\beta}}_{0}
,
where we let
{\bm{\beta}}_{0}
denote the regression coefficient of the true
conditional density.

We assume the following regularity conditions.

(C1)

The function
c(\cdot)\in C^{q}([0,1])
where
q\geq 1
and
c(0)=0
.
The true conditional density of
Y
given
{\bf X}
,
f_{Y|{\bf X}}(y|{\bf x})
,
has a compact support
[0,1]
, is positive and bounded on its support.
The marginal density of
{\bf X}
,
f_{{\bf X}}({\bf x})
,
has compact support
\mathcal{X}

and is bounded on its support.

(C2)

The spline order
r\geq q
.

(C3)

Define the knots

t_{-r+1}=\dots=t_{0}=0<t_{1}<\dots<t_{N}<1=t_{N+1}=\dots=t_{N+r}

where N is the number of interior knots
and [0,1] is divided into
(N+1)
subintervals.
Let
m=N+r
.
N satisfies
N\xrightarrow{}\infty
,

N^{-q}n^{1/2}\xrightarrow{}0
,
and
N^{-1}n(\hbox{log}n)^{-1}\xrightarrow{}\infty
as
n\xrightarrow{}\infty
.

(C4)

Let
h_{p}
be the distance between the
p
th and
(p+1)
th interior knots,

h=\max_{r\leq p\leq N+r}h_{p}
and
h^{\prime}=\min_{r\leq p\leq N+r}h_{p}
.
There exists a constant
C_{h}
such that
0<C_{h}<\infty
and
h/h^{\prime}<C_{h}
.
Therefore,
h\asymp N^{-1}
and
h^{\prime}\asymp N^{-1}
.

(C5)

(
De Boor 1978
)
Under Conditions (C1)-(C4),
there exists a spline coefficient
{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}

with the first component
\gamma_{01}=0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in[0,1]}\left|{\bf B}(y)^{\rm T}{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}-c(y)\right|=O(h^{q}).

(C6)

{\bf\Sigma}_{22}\equiv E[\hbox{var}\{{\bf B}(Y)|{\bf X}\}]
is invertible
and
\|{\bf\Sigma}_{22}\|_{2}^{-1}{\bf\Sigma}_{22}
has all eigenvalues bounded above
C_{l}
, where
C_{l}>0
is a constant.

Condition
(C1)
imposes the smoothness of the functional
component
c(\cdot)
,
and the boundedness of the densities involved in the model, which are
standard requirements.
The compact support requirement of the densities is also a standard
requirement in the B-spline literature.
Condition
(C2)
requires that the order of the B-spline basis is
sufficiently large
for the B-spline curve to converge to the true function fast enough.
We further assume that there are an appropriate number of interior
knots by Condition
(C3)
,
and that the knots are uniformly distributed in the asymptotic sense by
Condition
(C4)
.
Lastly, we point out that Condition
(C5)
does not further
impose any additional requirement. It is a direct
result given Conditions
(C1)
-
(C4)
,
and is only stated as a condition for convenience.

Proposition 1
.

Under Conditions
(C1)
-
(C6)
,

\|\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}({\bm{\beta}}_{0})-{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}\|_{2}=O_{p}\{(nh)^{-1/2}\}

and

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}({\bm{\beta}}_{0})-{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bf\Sigma}_{22}^{-1}n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[{\bf B}(y_{i})-E\{{\bf B}(Y)|{\bf x}_{i}\}]+{\bf r}_{1},

where
\|{\bf r}_{1}\|_{2}=o_{p}\{(nh)^{-1/2}\}
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in[0,1]}|\widehat{c}(y,{\bm{\beta}}_{0})-c(y)|=O_{p}\{(nh)^{-1/2}+h^{q}\}.

Proposition
1
states that given the true regression
coefficient
{\bm{\beta}}_{0}
,

\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}
converges to the B-spline basis coefficient
{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}

at the nonparametric convergence rate
(nh)^{-1/2}
,
hence the B-spline curve approximates
c(\cdot)
.
We further obtain that
the estimator
\widehat{c}(\cdot,{\bm{\beta}}_{0})
converges to the true function

c(\cdot)
at the
(nh)^{-1/2}
rate as well.
Based on Proposition
1
,
below we further establish the asymptotic property
of the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
. Note
{\bm{\beta}}
is not our
research interest, hence this property is stated as a by-product.
We first impose one additional regularity condition, which is a
standard requirement.

(C7)

The expectation of the conditional covariance of
\{{\bf X}^{\rm T}Y,{\bf B}^{\rm T}(Y)\}^{\rm T}
given
{\bf X}
, i.e.

\displaystyle{\bf\Sigma}\equiv\begin{bmatrix}{\bf\Sigma}_{11}&{\bf\Sigma}_{12}\\
{\bf\Sigma}_{21}&{\bf\Sigma}_{22}\end{bmatrix}\equiv E\begin{bmatrix}{\bf X}{\bf X}^{\rm T}\hbox{var}(Y|{\bf X})&{\bf X}\hbox{cov}\{Y,{\bf B}(Y)|{\bf X}\}\\
\hbox{cov}\{{\bf B}(Y),Y|{\bf X}\}{\bf X}^{\rm T}&\hbox{var}\{{\bf B}(Y)|{\bf X}\}\end{bmatrix},

is invertible.

It is easy to see that
the conditional covariance of
\{{\bf X}^{\rm T}Y,{\bf B}^{\rm T}(Y)\}^{\rm T}

given
{\bf X}
is positive semidefinite.
Thus,
{\bf\Sigma}
is also positive semidefinite.
Condition
(C7)
further requires that
{\bf\Sigma}
is positive
definite, which is very mild.
Note that this guarantees

{\bf\Sigma}_{11}
and the Schur complement of
{\bf\Sigma}_{22}
,

{\bf\Sigma}^{*}\equiv{\bf\Sigma}_{11}-{\bf\Sigma}_{12}{\bf\Sigma}_{22}^{-1}{\bf\Sigma}_{21}
,
are both positive definite.

Proposition 2
.

Under Conditions
(C1)
-
(C7)
,

\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}-{\bm{\beta}}_{0}\|_{2}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
and

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\beta}}-{\bm{\beta}}_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bf\Sigma}^{*-1}n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bf x}_{i}\{y_{i}-E(Y|{\bf x}_{i})\}

\displaystyle-{\bf\Sigma}^{*-1}{\bf\Sigma}_{12}{\bf\Sigma}_{22}^{-1}n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[{\bf B}(y_{i})-E\{{\bf B}(Y)|{\bf x}_{i}\}]+{\bf r}_{2},

where
\|{\bf r}_{2}\|_{2}=o_{p}(n^{-1/2})
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle{\bf\Sigma}^{*1/2}\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}-{\bm{\beta}}_{0})\to N({\bf 0}_{p},{\bf I}_{p})

in distribution as
n\to\infty
.

Proposition
2
establishes how the regression coefficient estimator

\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
is asymptotically distributed,
and allows to perform inference
since the asymptotic variance of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
is estimable based on

\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
and
f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y,{\bf x},\widehat{\bm{\beta}},\widehat{c})
.
Below, we study how the estimator of the marginal effect
{\bm{\xi}}

is asymptotically distributed in Theorem
1
.
We point out that the asymptotic variance of
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}
is estimable as well
so that we can perform inference on the marginal effect.

Theorem 1
.

Let
{\bf\Sigma}_{\bm{\xi}}\equiv{\bf A}{\bf\Sigma}^{-1}{\bf A}^{\rm T}+{\bm{\beta}}_{0}{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{\rm T}\hbox{var}\{\hbox{var}(Y|{\bf X})\}
,
where
{\bf A}\equiv[{\bf A}_{1},{\bf A}_{2}]
and

\displaystyle{\bf A}_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle E\{\hbox{var}(Y|{\bf X})\}{\bf I}+{\bm{\beta}}_{0}E[\{Y-E(Y|{\bf X})\}^{3}{\bf X}^{\rm T}],

\displaystyle{\bf A}_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle{\bm{\beta}}_{0}E(\{Y-E(Y|{\bf X})\}^{2}[{\bf B}(Y)-E\{{\bf B}(Y)|{\bf X}\}])^{\rm T}.

Under Conditions
(C1)
-
(C7)
,

\displaystyle{\bf\Sigma}_{\bm{\xi}}^{-1/2}\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\bm{\xi}}-{\bm{\xi}}_{0})\to N({\bf 0}_{p},{\bf I}_{p})

in distribution as
n\to\infty
.

To facilitate the analysis of the properties of the quantile effect
estimator
\widehat{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
,
we assume two additional regularity conditions.

(C8)

c^{\prime\prime}(\cdot)
is bounded on
[0,1]
.

(C9)

The spline order
r\geq 2
.

Note that for an arbitrary twice differentiable function
g(\cdot)
,

\|g^{\prime}(\cdot)\|_{\infty}\leq 2(\|g(\cdot)\|_{\infty}\|g^{\prime\prime}(\cdot)\|_{\infty})^{1/2}

by the Landau-Kolmogorov inequality.
Setting
g(\cdot)={\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}-c(\cdot)
,
Conditions
(C5)
,
(C8)
, and
(C9)
guarantee

\|{\bf B}^{\prime}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}-c^{\prime}(\cdot)\|_{\infty}=O(h^{q/2})
, i.e.

{\bf B}^{\prime}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}
converges to
c^{\prime}(\cdot)
uniformly at the
rate
O(h^{q/2})
.
Theorem
2
below provides
the asymptotic normality of the quantile effect estimator
\widehat{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
.

Theorem 2
.

Let
{\bf\Sigma}_{{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}}\equiv{\bf C}{\bf\Sigma}^{-1}{\bf C}^{\rm T}+{\bm{\beta}}_{0}{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{\rm T}\hbox{var}\{q^{\prime}({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0})\}
,
where
{\bf C}\equiv[{\bf C}_{1},{\bf C}_{2}]
and

\displaystyle{\bf C}_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle E\{q^{\prime}({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0})\}{\bf I}+{\bm{\beta}}_{0}E\Bigg[{\bf X}^{\rm T}\Bigg\{\frac{E([\tau-I\{Y\leq q({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0})\}]Y^{2}|{\bf X})}{f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}\{q({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0})|{\bf X}\}}

\displaystyle-2q^{\prime}({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0})q({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0})-\{q^{\prime}({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0})\}^{2}[{\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0}+c^{\prime}\{q({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0})\}]\}],

\displaystyle{\bf C}_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle{\bm{\beta}}_{0}E\Bigg\{\frac{E([\tau-I\{Y\leq q({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0})\}]Y{\bf B}^{\rm T}(Y)|{\bf X})}{f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}\{q({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0})|{\bf X}\}}

\displaystyle-q^{\prime}({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0}){\bf B}^{\rm T}\{q({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0})\}-\frac{E([\tau-I\{Y\leq q({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0})\}]{\bf B}^{\rm T}(Y)|{\bf X})}{f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}\{q({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0})|{\bf X}\}}

\displaystyle\times[q({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0})+q^{\prime}({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0}){\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0}+q^{\prime}({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0})c^{\prime}\{q({\bf X}^{\rm T}{\bm{\beta}}_{0})\}]\}.

Under Conditions
(C1)
-
(C9)
,

\displaystyle{\bf\Sigma}_{{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}}^{-1/2}\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}-{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau 0})\to N({\bf 0}_{p},{\bf I}_{p})

in distribution as
n\to\infty
.

The asymptotic properties we established, especially the estimation variances of
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
,
have very different forms from the efficiency bounds we derived in
Sections
2.1
and
2.2
. Nevertheless,
closer inspection, together with some basic but less frequently
adopted linear algebra tools reveal that these two sets of results
have much closer connections, and
the efficiency bounds in estimating both
{\bm{\xi}}
and
{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
are
actually reached by our B-spline based approximate maximum likelihood
estimators. As a by-product,
we also state the efficiency property of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
as a proposition,
even though our interest is not in
{\bm{\beta}}
.

Proposition 3
.

Under Conditions
(C1)
-
(C7)
,
the approximate maximum likelihood estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
is efficient.

Theorem 3
.

Under Conditions
(C1)
-
(C7)
,
the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}
based on
the approximate maximum likelihood estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
and
\widehat{c}(\cdot)
is efficient.

Theorem 4
.

Under Conditions
(C1)
-
(C9)
,
the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
based on
the approximate maximum likelihood estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
and
\widehat{c}(\cdot)
is efficient.

3.2
Discrete Response

We now analyze the properties of our estimators
under the discrete response case.
For notational simplicity, we denote

{\bm{\theta}}\equiv({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T},{\bm{\gamma}}^{\rm T})^{\rm T}
,

{\bf p}({\bf x},{\bm{\theta}})\equiv\{\hbox{pr}(Y=1\mid{\bf x};{\bm{\beta}},{\bf c}),\dots,\hbox{pr}(Y=m\mid{\bf x};{\bm{\beta}},{\bf c})\}^{\rm T}
,
and
{\bf p}_{k}({\bf x},{\bm{\theta}})\equiv\{1^{k}\hbox{pr}(Y=1\mid{\bf x};{\bm{\beta}},{\bf c}),\dots,m^{k}\hbox{pr}(Y=m\mid{\bf x};{\bm{\beta}},{\bf c})\}^{\rm T}
.
Also in this section, we use
{\bf B}(y)
to denote a vector of indicator functions,
i.e.,
{\bf B}(y)\equiv\{I(y=1),\dots,I(y=m)\}^{\rm T}
. Note that we allow

m
to grow with the sample size
n
.
Here we present theoretical results when
m
grows to infinity.
Note that in the finite
m
case, the analysis can be done easily
through incorporating the classical maximum likelihood approach.
We first list a set of regularity conditions.

(D1)

The true conditional mass function of
Y
given
{\bf X}
,
\hbox{pr}(Y=y\mid{\bf x};{\bm{\theta}}_{0})
,
has a support set
\{0,\dots,m\}
.

E\{\hbox{pr}(Y=0\mid{\bf X};{\bm{\theta}}_{0})\}\leq 1-\delta
for some constant
0<\delta<1

and
E(Y^{4}\mid{\bf x})
is bounded.
The marginal density of
{\bf X}
,
f_{{\bf X}}({\bf x})
,
has compact support
\mathcal{X}
and is bounded on its support.

(D2)

There exist constants
L_{k}
such that

|E(Y^{k}\mid{\bf x},{\bm{\theta}}^{*})-E(Y^{k}\mid{\bf x},{\bm{\theta}}_{0})|\leq L_{k}\|{\bm{\theta}}^{*}-{\bm{\theta}}_{0}\|_{2}

for
k=1,2,3
.

(D3)

m\to\infty
,
n^{-1}m^{3}\to 0
, and
E\{\|{\bf p}({\bf X},{\bm{\theta}}_{0})\|_{2}^{2}\}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

(D4)

{\bf\Sigma}_{22}\equiv E[\hbox{var}\{{\bf B}(Y)\mid{\bf X}\}]
is invertible.

\|{\bf\Sigma}_{22}\|_{2}^{-1}{\bf\Sigma}_{22}
has all eigenvalues bounded above a
constant
C_{l}>0
.

(D5)

The expectation of the conditional covariance of
\{{\bf X}^{\rm T}Y,{\bf B}^{\rm T}(Y)\}^{\rm T}
given
{\bf X}
, i.e.

\displaystyle{\bf\Sigma}\equiv\begin{bmatrix}{\bf\Sigma}_{11}&{\bf\Sigma}_{12}\\
{\bf\Sigma}_{21}&{\bf\Sigma}_{22}\end{bmatrix}\equiv E\begin{bmatrix}{\bf X}{\bf X}^{\rm T}\hbox{var}(Y\mid{\bf X})&{\bf X}\hbox{cov}\{Y,{\bf B}(Y)\mid{\bf X}\}\\
\hbox{cov}\{{\bf B}(Y),Y\mid{\bf X}\}{\bf X}^{\rm T}&\hbox{var}\{{\bf B}(Y)\mid{\bf X}\}\end{bmatrix},

is invertible.

Conditions
(D1)
and
(D2)
require
boundedness and Lipschitz continuity on the conditional moments of
Y
,
which are standard requirements.
Condition
(D3)
requires
m
to tend to infinity
at the rate slower than
n^{1/3}
,
and the mass function not to concentrate on a finite subset of the support.
Further, since
{\bf\Sigma}_{22}
and
{\bf\Sigma}
are positive semidefinite by their definitions,
the invertibility imposed by Conditions
(D4)
and
(D5)
is very mild.
Lastly, we point out some results on
{\bf\Sigma}_{22}
in Remark

1
below.

Remark 1
.

Note that the sum of the eigenvalues of
{\bf\Sigma}_{22}
is of constant order by
Conditions
(D1)
and
(D3)
, because

\hbox{trace}({\bf\Sigma}_{22})=E\left\{1-\hbox{pr}(Y=0\mid{\bf X};{\bm{\theta}}_{0})-\|{\bf p}({\bf X},\theta_{0})\|_{2}^{2}\right\}
.
Thus we get
\|{\bf\Sigma}_{22}\|_{2}\asymp m^{-1}
and

\|{\bf\Sigma}_{22}^{-1}\|_{2}\asymp m

because the eigenvalues are of the same order by Condition
(D4)
.

We now state the convergence rate and the asymptotic properties
of the estimators of the model parameters
{\bm{\beta}}
and
{\bm{\gamma}}
.
Our analysis shows that the estimator of the regression coefficient
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}

achieves the parametric convergence rate
under the regularity conditions stated above.
Further, we establish the asymptotic distribution of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}

as a by-product,
by which one can perform inference on the regression coefficient.
We formally state the results in Proposition
4
below.

Proposition 4
.

Under Conditions
(D1)
-
(D5)
,

\|\widehat{\bm{\beta}}-{\bm{\beta}}_{0}\|_{2}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2})
,

\|\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}-{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}\|_{2}=O_{p}(n^{-1/2}m^{1/2})
, and

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\widehat{\bm{\beta}}-{\bm{\beta}}_{0}\\
\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}-{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}\end{bmatrix}={\bf\Sigma}^{-1}n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\begin{bmatrix}{\bf x}_{i}\{y_{i}-E(Y\mid{\bf x}_{i})\}\\
{\bf B}(y_{i})-E\{{\bf B}(Y)\mid{\bf x}_{i}\}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}{\bf r}_{1}\\
{\bf r}_{2}\end{bmatrix},

where
\|{\bf r}_{1}\|_{2}=o_{p}(n^{-1/2})
and
\|{\bf r}_{2}\|_{2}=o_{p}(n^{-1/2}m^{1/2})
.
Furthermore, let
{\bf\Sigma}_{\bm{\beta}}\equiv({\bf\Sigma}_{11}-{\bf\Sigma}_{12}{\bf\Sigma}_{22}^{-1}{\bf\Sigma}_{21})^{-1}
, then

\displaystyle{\bf\Sigma}_{\bm{\beta}}^{-1/2}\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\bm{\beta}}-{\bm{\beta}}_{0})\to N({\bf 0}_{p},{\bf I}_{p})

in distribution as
n\to\infty
.

Based on Proposition
4
,
we further establish a theoretical result on the marginal effect estimator,
which states the convergence rate and the asymptotic distribution of
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}
.
Theorem
5
shows that
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}
,
the functional of both the parametric and the nonparametric components,
achieves the parametric convergence rate.
We also provide the closed form of the asymptotic variance of
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}
,
which can be used to infer the marginal effect of a population.

Theorem 5
.

Let
{\bf\Sigma}_{\bm{\xi}}\equiv{\bf A}{\bf\Sigma}^{-1}{\bf A}^{\rm T}+{\bm{\beta}}_{0}{\bm{\beta}}_{0}^{\rm T}\hbox{var}\{\hbox{var}(Y\mid{\bf X})\}
,
where
{\bf A}\equiv[{\bf A}_{1},{\bf A}_{2}]
and

\displaystyle{\bf A}_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle E\{\hbox{var}(Y\mid{\bf X})\}{\bf I}+{\bm{\beta}}_{0}E[\{Y-E(Y\mid{\bf X})\}^{3}{\bf X}^{\rm T}],

\displaystyle{\bf A}_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle{\bm{\beta}}_{0}E(\{Y-E(Y\mid{\bf X})\}^{2}[{\bf B}(Y)-E\{{\bf B}(Y)\mid{\bf X}\}])^{\rm T}.

Under Conditions
(D1)
-
(D5)
,

\displaystyle{\bf\Sigma}_{\bm{\xi}}^{-1/2}\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\bm{\xi}}-{\bm{\xi}}_{0})\to N({\bf 0}_{p},{\bf I}_{p})

in distribution as
n\to\infty
.

We now show that our proposed estimators

\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}
for the discrete case also
achieve the efficiency bounds.
Although the asymptotic variances of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}

established in Proposition
4
and
in Theorem
5

appear very different from the efficiency bounds derived
in Appendix
S.9
and Section
S.1
,
our analysis shows that these two
seemingly different variance structures are actually identical.
Below, we formally state the efficiency of
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}
as Theorem

6
,
and that of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
as Proposition
5
.

Proposition 5
.

Under Conditions
(D1)
-
(D5)
,
the approximate maximum likelihood estimator
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
is efficient.

Theorem 6
.

Under Conditions
(D1)
-
(D5)
,
the estimator
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}
based on the approximate maximum likelihood estimator

\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
and
\widehat{c}(\cdot)
is efficient.

4
Simulation Experiments

We conduct simulation studies to investigate the finite sample
performance of the proposed methods. We consider the case where the
response follows a GLM or a truncated GLM.
All results are based on 1000 replicates with sample size
n=1000
.
For comparison, we implemented three different estimators,
our proposed approximate maximum likelihood estimator (aMLE),
the pairwise marginal likelihood estimator (pMLE) by
Lin et al. 2021
,
and the maximum likelihood estimator (MLE) under the non-truncated
regression model.
Note that MLE is the most efficient estimator if the response

Y
is not truncated, but wrongly specifies the distribution when the
response
Y
is actually truncated.

For our proposed aMLE for the continuous response,
we used the cubic B-spline basis
with the number of interior knots equal to
the smallest integer larger than
0.7n^{1/5}
, i.e.,
N=\lceil 0.7n^{1/5}\rceil
,
where the knots are the quantiles of
\{y_{i}:i=1,\dots,n\}
of length
(N+2)

whose levels are evenly spaced in
[0,1]
.
On the other hand, because
Lin et al. 2021
only studied the estimation
of
{\bm{\beta}}
and
c(\cdot)
,
we implemented the estimators
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
of pMLE
in the same manner as for aMLE, based on the pMLE
estimated
{\bm{\beta}}
and
c(\cdot)
.

We report the average of the absolute bias,
the sample standard error
\sigma_{\text{sim}}
,
the average of the asymptotic standard error
\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{est}}
,
and the empirical coverage of the estimated confidence interval at
95% confidence level (CI).
The
\widehat{\sigma}_{\text{est}}
and CI of pMLE are omitted
because
Lin et al. 2021
did not provide them.

4.1
Normal Distribution

We first examine the normal regression model.
A three dimensional covariate vector
{\bf X}_{i}
was
independently drawn from a multivariate normal distribution with mean
{\bf 0}

and covariance
\Sigma=(\sigma_{kl})
where

\sigma_{kl}=0.1^{|k-l|},k=1,\dots,3,l=1,\dots,3
.
Then for the truncated case, we further generated a response
Y_{i}

independently
from a truncated normal distribution on
[a,b]
, which has the density

\displaystyle f_{Y}(y;\theta_{i},\sigma,a,b)=\frac{\frac{1}{\sigma}\phi(\frac{y-\theta_{i}}{\sigma})}{\Phi(\frac{b-\theta_{i}}{\sigma})-\Phi(\frac{a-\theta_{i}}{\sigma})},

where
\theta_{i}={\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}_{i},\sigma=1,a=-5,b=5
,

\phi(\cdot)
and
\Phi(\cdot)
are the probability density function
(pdf) and the cumulative distribution function (cdf) of the
standard normal distribution.
We set
{\bm{\beta}}=(1,2,3)^{\rm T}
in the simulation procedure.
For the non-truncated case, we generated replicates with the same
parameters but without truncation.
The non-truncated simulation design is identical to that of
Lin et al. 2021
.

To evaluate the performance under the truncated case, we illustrate
in Figure
1(a)

how the estimated
c(\cdot)
performed by our method while fixing
c(-5)=0
.
As can be seen from the plot,
\widehat{c}(\cdot)
approximated

c(\cdot)
with satisfactory bias and variance at sample size
1000. This reflects the theoretical properties described in Lemma

6
of the supplementary material.
In addition, in Table
1
, we illustrate the estimation
properties for
{\bm{\beta}}
and
{\bm{\xi}}
via aMLE, pMLE and MLE.
The aMLE and pMLE methods were numerically almost identical in terms of
both bias and standard error, reflecting the theoretical properties
established in Proposition
3
as well as those in Theorem 1
of
Lin et al. 2021
. In terms of inference of aMLE, the estimated
standard error was very close to the sample standard error, and
the coverage rate of the confidence intervals was
close to the nominal level
95\%
. These indicate that our asymptotic
properties are already useful at sample size
n=1000
in this
model. In contrast, the inference results of MLE were very bad, with
the coverage rate almost 0. This is a direct consequence of the
estimation bias caused by model misspecification.
Table
S.1
in the supplementary material
provides the estimation results of
{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}

at the quantile levels
\tau=0.05,0.25,0.5,0.75
and 0.95
respectively. Again, the estimation and inference properties of aMLE
were satisfactory, suggesting that the properties described in
Theorem
2
is reflected in this model for
n=1000
.
Also, MLE performed poorly in estimating
{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
especially at the
low or high quantile level.

For the non-truncated case, the corresponding results are shown in
Figure
1(b)
and Tables
2
and

S.2
in the supplementary material.
We can see from Figure
1(b)

that
\widehat{c}(\cdot)
estimated the curve
c(\cdot)
sufficiently well,
even though the response is infinitely supported hence our compact
support assumption is violated. We also note the small biases of
the estimators
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
,
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}
, and
\widehat{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
from
Tables
2
and
S.2
.
Moreover, interestingly,
our estimators of
{\bm{\xi}}
and
{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
performed as well as MLE in
this simulation setting, even when
\tau
is near 0 or 1. In terms of
inference, the empirical coverage of the estimated confidence
interval by our method was close to the nominal level. These seem to
suggest an empirical robustness property of our aMLE against the
compact support assumption.
Lastly, we point out that our method aMLE was more efficient than pMLE
in this example in terms of estimating
{\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\xi}}
as well as the quantile
effect
{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
at various quantile levels
\tau
.

4.2
Gamma Distribution

Next, we consider the situation when
Y
given
{\bf x}
has a gamma
distribution.
We first generated a covariate vector
{\bf X}_{i}=(X_{1i},X_{2i})^{\rm T}
,
where
X_{ki},k=1,2
are independently and identically distributed
(iid) as a uniform random variable on
[0.5,1]
.
Then for the truncated case, a response
Y_{i}
was generated independently
from a truncated gamma distribution with the pdf

\displaystyle f_{Y|{\bf X}}(y;\alpha,\theta_{i},b)=\frac{y^{\alpha-1}e^{-y/\theta_{i}}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\theta_{i}^{\alpha}}\left\{\int_{0}^{b}\frac{y^{\alpha-1}e^{-y/\theta_{i}}}{\Gamma(\alpha)\theta_{i}^{\alpha}}\right\}^{-1},

where
\alpha=5
,
\alpha\theta_{i}=1/{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}_{i}
,
b=2
, and

{\bm{\beta}}=(0.5,1)^{\rm T}
.
For the non-truncated case, we simply carried out the same data
generation mechanism while applying the usual gamma distribution
with the same parameters.

For the truncated case,
Figure
1(c)
illustrates the performance of the estimator
\widehat{c}(\cdot)
with
c(2)=0
. The estimation had very small bias on
most part of the support except when
y
is
close to 0 due to the boundary effect.
Indeed, the true curve
c(y)=(\alpha-1)\hbox{log}(y)
is unbounded near 0
and very few observations are available since the density converges to
0. Nevertheless, we can see from Tables
3
and

4

that aMLE estimated
{\bm{\beta}}
,
{\bm{\xi}}
, and
{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
well with small
bias, and its inference was also sufficiently precise with a good match
between the sample variance and the estimated variance, and the 95%
confidence intervals had coverage close to the nominal level. In
contrast, MLE showed bias and did not perform well in general, and
deteriorated
further when
\tau
is near 1.
In our application, we also find that
pMLE was computationally unstable in this setting and did not lead
to reasonable results.

For the case when the response is not truncated,
Figure
1(d)
shows that similar performance was observed
as in the truncated gamma regression case. In addition,
Tables
5
and
S.3
in the supplementary
material suggest that
aMLE still provided good results in estimating the
corresponding parameters and can be used for reliable inference.
In this situation, aMLE still outperformed pMLE numerically
even though both should be inconsistent in theory. Because MLE assumes a
fully parametric model which happens to be correct, it had the best
performance among all three methods as we expected.

4.3
Bernoulli Distribution

Our first simulation for the discrete response was carried out
under a conditional Bernoulli
distribution with the intention to investigate the performance of aMLE
in the
discrete response case.
A three dimensional covariate vector
{\bf X}_{i}
was generated
independently from a multivariate normal distribution with mean
{\bf 0}

and covariance
\Sigma=(\sigma_{kl})
where
\sigma_{kl}=0.1^{|k-l|},k=1,\dots,3,l=1,\dots,3
.
Then a binary response
Y_{i}
was drawn independently
from a Bernoulli distribution with a success rate
1/\{1+\exp(-{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}_{i})\}
.
We set
{\bm{\beta}}=(-0.5,0.5,1)^{\rm T}
.

The performances of the estimators
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}
by the three methods
are given in Table
6
.
All methods performed similarly to each other,
because the problem is fully parametric as explained in Section
2.3
.

4.4
Poisson and Negative Binomial Distributions

We now conduct simulation studies for the case where

Y
is discrete with infinite support. Specifically,
Y
has
Poisson and negative binomial distributions respectively.
We first generated a covariate vector
{\bf X}_{i}=(X_{1i},X_{2i})^{\rm T}
,
where
X_{ki},k=1,2
are independently and identically distributed
(iid) uniform random variables on
[0.5,1]
.
Then for the Poisson regression model, a response
Y_{i}
was generated
from a Poisson distribution with the rate

\theta_{i}=\exp({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}_{i})
where
{\bm{\beta}}=(0,1)^{\rm T}
.
For the negative binomial case,
we generated
Y_{i}
independently from a negative binomial distribution with the mass function

\displaystyle\hbox{pr}(Y=y;r,\theta_{i})={y+r-1\choose r-1}\theta_{i}^{y}(1-\theta_{i})^{r},

where
r=2
,
\theta_{i}=\exp({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}_{i})
, and
{\bm{\beta}}=(0,-1)^{\rm T}
.
In estimation,
MLE assumes
\theta_{i}=\exp({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}_{i})
and
c(y)=-\hbox{log}(y!)

hence results in the most efficient estimator under the Poission regression
among the three methods,
but wrongly specifies the structure
when the conditional distribution is the negative binomial.

Tables
7
and
8
show the
{\bm{\beta}}
and
{\bm{\xi}}
estimation results
under the Poisson and the negative binomial regression models respectively.
In both cases, we found that aMLE estimated
{\bm{\xi}}
more efficiently than pMLE
even though our method aMLE showed similar performance to pMLE in estimating
{\bm{\beta}}
.
Especially for the estimation of
\xi_{2}
in Table
8
,
we can see that aMLE was about twice as efficient as pMLE
in terms of both bias and standard error.
This supports that our estimation procedure is more suitable for estimating
{\bm{\xi}}

in finite sample situations than the method proposed by
Lin et al. 2021
.
Also, considering that the empirical coverage of our approximate confidence interval
reached the nominal level of 95%,
the results confirm the asymptotic properties of
\widehat{\bm{\beta}}
and
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}

proposed in Proposition
4
and Theorem
5
.
On the other hand, MLE in Table
7
indeed had the best
performance in terms of both bias and variance, benefiting from the
perfectly correct parametric model setting.
However, we can observe from Table
8
that
the estimators from MLE were severely biased when
c(y)
was misspecified.

5
Swiss Non-labor Income Data Analysis

We now analyze a data set concerning non-labor income situation
in Switzerland.
The data set is publicly available from the R-package
AER

(
Kleiber & Zeileis 2008
)
,
and consists of information collected from 871 married
women randomly
drawn from the representative health survey for Switzerland (SOMIPOPS)
in 1981 with income not abnormally low.

Our goal is to estimate the marginal effect and the quantile effect of
covariates on
non-labor income (such as husband’s income). Thus, the non-labor
income in the log scale is used as response
Y
.
All the other variables in the dataset are considered as explanatory
variables, and they include
Participation (taking the value 1 if the individual participated
in the labor market, 0 otherwise),
Age (age in years),
Age2 (squared age in years then divided by 10),
Education (years of formal education),
Foreign (taking the value 1 if the individual is a permanent foreign
resident, 0 otherwise),
Youngkids (number of young children),
Oldkids (number of older children).
The age category for children was decided by whether the age is under 7.
For more detailed description, see
Gerfin 1996
.

We implemented our method aMLE. For comparison, we also implemented
the normal and the gamma regression.
We fitted the models without any transformation on or
interaction between the covariates, while
the number of knots of the B-spline basis is chosen in the same way as
explained in Section
4
.

In Figure
2
, we can see that
the fitted distribution from aMLE does not resemble that of the normal
regression, whose
c(y)=-y^{2}/2\sigma^{2}
with
\sigma>0
. It also does
not resemble that of the gamma distribution whose
c(y)=(\alpha-1)\hbox{log}(y)
with
\alpha>0
. In Table
9
, we compared
the information criteria AIC and BIC of the three models, and found
that our aMLE on (
4
) has the lowest AIC and BIC compared
to the normal and
the gamma regression model, hence is the most suitable modeling choice.
This suggests that our model in (
4
) is the most suitable
to use in analyzing the swiss non-labor income data.

Table
10
shows the inference result in estimating the marginal effect.
All except one explanatory variables have similar levels of
significance based on all three methods. The exception is “Foreign”,
which was selected as a significant variable at 5%
level in the analysis based on aMLE, but was considered non-significant
in both the normal and the gamma regression.
In other words, only by using (
4
) in combination with aMLE,
we can conclude that being a
permanent foreign resident has a negative effect on the non-labor income
in Switzerland.

In Figure
3
, we further illustrate the estimated
\widehat{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}

at the quantile level
\tau=0.05
, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75 and 0.95.
We fixed the significance level at 5%.
The p-values of
\widehat{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
were largely similar to those of
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}
.
However, as we can see from the plot,
the magnitude of the quantile effect increases when
\tau
is near 0 or 1.
That is, our model suggests that
when the non-labor income level is relatively low or high,
the effect of the covariates on the non-labor income is more extreme.

6
Discussion

We have proposed a B-spline based approximate maximum likelihood
estimation procedure to estimate both the marginal effect and the quantile
effect in a semiparametric generalized linear model. Compared to the
classical GLM, our model is more flexible hence less susceptible to
model misspecification.
The estimators we proposed are shown to reach the
semiparametric efficiency bounds, hence are optimal.

Following the spirit of GLMs, we have worked with a linear summary
of covariates
{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}
in our work. It is easy to see that we
can replace
{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}
by a more general form
m({\bf x},{\bm{\beta}})
, where

m
is a known function which can be nonlinear. All our procedures can
be carried through while replacing
{\bf x}
by
\partial m({\bf x},{\bm{\beta}})/\partial{\bm{\beta}}
,
where we only need to ensure identifiability, sufficient smoothness,
and boundedness of the corresponding quantities to facilitate the
almost identical derivation to the linear case.

An interesting but difficult further extension of our work worth
mentioning is the possibility of allowing non-compactly
supported distribution of
Y
.
Although truncation is
routinely done in practice to bypass the complexity, we found it very difficult
to rigorously extend our
theoretical analysis to encompass this scenario. We suspect that
more
fundamental work is needed, possibly in a model simpler than the
semiparametric GLM proposed in (
4
).

SUPPLEMENTARY MATERIAL

S.1
Derivation of the Efficiency Bound of Marginal Effect Estimation

Consider an arbitrary parametric submodel

\displaystyle f_{{\bf X},Y}(y,{\bf x},{\bm{\theta}})=f_{{\bf X}}({\bf x},{\bm{\alpha}})\frac{\exp\left\{y{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}+c(y,{\bm{\gamma}})\right\}}{\int\exp\left\{y{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}+c(y,{\bm{\gamma}})\right\}d\mu(y)},

(S.1)

where
{\bm{\theta}}=({\bm{\alpha}}^{\rm T},{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T},{\bm{\gamma}}^{\rm T})^{\rm T}
. We can
verify that the score functions associated with an arbitrary
{\bm{\theta}}
are

{\bf S}_{\bm{\alpha}}({\bf x})={\bf a}({\bf x})
, where
{\bf a}({\bf x})
can be any function that satisfies

E\{{\bf a}({\bf X})\}={\bf 0}
,

{\bf S}_{\bm{\beta}}(y,{\bf x})={\bf x}\{y-E(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})\}
,
and
{\bf S}_{\bm{\gamma}}(y,{\bf x})={\bf a}(y)-E\{{\bf a}(Y)\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}\}
, where
{\bf a}(y)
can
be any function.
We can verify that

{\bm{\xi}}={\bm{\beta}}E\{v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}
and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\bm{\xi}}}{\partial{\bm{\alpha}}^{\rm T}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bm{\beta}}E\{v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}){\bf a}^{\rm T}({\bf X})\},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\bm{\xi}}}{\partial{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\{v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}{\bf I}+{\bm{\beta}}E[{\bf X}^{\rm T}\{Y-E(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}^{3}],

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\bm{\xi}}}{\partial{\bm{\gamma}}^{\rm T}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bm{\beta}}E\left([{\bf a}(Y)-E\{{\bf a}(Y)\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X}\}]^{\rm T}\{Y-E(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}^{2}\right).

Thus, following
Bickel et al. 1998
and
Tsiatis 2006
, a
possible influence function is

\displaystyle\bm{\phi}(y,{\bf x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bm{\beta}}v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})-{\bm{\beta}}E\{v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}+{\bm{\beta}}\{y-E(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})\}^{2}

\displaystyle-{\bm{\beta}}E[\{Y-E(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})\}^{2}\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}]+{\bf B}E\{v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}[{\bf b}(y,{\bf x})-E\{{\bf b}(Y,{\bf x})\mid{\bf x}\}],

where

\displaystyle{\bf B}=\left(E[\{Y-E(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}{\bf b}(Y,{\bf X}){\bf X}^{\rm T}]\right)^{-1},

(S.2)

and
{\bf b}(y,{\bf x})
is such that

\displaystyle E\{{\bf b}(y,{\bf X})\mid y\}=E[E\{{\bf b}(Y,{\bf X})\mid{\bf X}\}\mid y].

(S.3)

We can verify that

\partial{\bm{\xi}}/\partial{\bm{\theta}}^{\rm T}=E(\bm{\phi}{\bf S}_{\bm{\theta}}^{\rm T})

where

{\bf S}_{\bm{\theta}}=({\bf S}_{\bm{\alpha}}^{\rm T},{\bf S}_{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T},{\bf S}_{\bm{\gamma}}^{\rm T})^{\rm T}
. Now,
the tangent space is
\cal T=\cal T_{\bm{\alpha}}\oplus(\cal T_{\bm{\beta}}+\cal T_{\bm{\gamma}})

where

\displaystyle\cal T_{\bm{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[{\bf a}({\bf x}):E\{{\bf a}({\bf X})\}={\bf 0}],

\displaystyle\cal T_{\bm{\beta}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[{\bf M}{\bf x}\{y-E(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})\}:\forall{\bf M}],

\displaystyle\cal T_{\bm{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[{\bf a}(y)-E\{{\bf a}(Y)\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}\}:\forall{\bf a}(y)].

Let

\displaystyle{\bf M}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\left({\bf B}E\{v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}{\bf b}(Y,{\bf X}){\bf X}^{\rm T}\{Y-E(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}-2{\bm{\beta}}{\bf X}^{\rm T}Yv({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\right.

(S.4)

\displaystyle\left.-{\bf a}(Y)\{Y-E(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}{\bf X}^{\rm T}\right)\left[E\{{\bf X}{\bf X}^{\rm T}v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}\right]^{-1},

where
{\bf a}(y)
is such that

\displaystyle E[E\{{\bf a}(Y)\mid{\bf X}\}\mid y]-{\bf a}(y)

(S.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2{\bm{\beta}}E[yE(Y\mid{\bf X})-E\{YE(Y\mid{\bf X})\mid{\bf X}\}\mid y]+{\bf M}E[{\bf X}\{y-E(Y\mid{\bf X})\}\mid y].

Then the efficient influence function is

\displaystyle\bm{\phi}_{\rm eff}(y,{\bf x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bm{\beta}}v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})-{\bm{\beta}}E\{v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}+{\bm{\beta}}\{y^{2}-E(Y^{2}\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})\}

\displaystyle+{\bf M}{\bf x}\{y-E(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})\}+{\bf a}(y)-E\{{\bf a}(Y)\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}\},

where
{\bf a}(y)
satisfies (
S.5
),
{\bf M}
is given in (
S.4
),

{\bf b}(y,{\bf x})
satisfies (
S.3
) and
{\bf B}
is given in (
S.2
).

Note that once we have
{\bf a}(y)
, we can let

{\bf b}(y,{\bf x})=2{\bm{\beta}}yE(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})+{\bf M}{\bf x}y+{\bf a}(y)
and it satisfies
(
S.3
). Using this specific
{\bf b}(y,{\bf x})
function,
we obtain that
{\bf M}
and
{\bf B}
need to satisfy

\displaystyle{\bf B}=\left(E[\{Y-E(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}\{2{\bm{\beta}}YE(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})+{\bf M}{\bf X}Y+{\bf a}(Y)\}{\bf X}^{\rm T}]\right)^{-1}

and

\displaystyle{\bf M}E\{{\bf X}{\bf X}^{\rm T}v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}=E\{v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}{\bf I}-E\left[2{\bm{\beta}}{\bf X}^{\rm T}Yv({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})+{\bf a}(Y)\{Y-E(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}{\bf X}^{\rm T}\right].

This leads to

\displaystyle{\bf M}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(E\{v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}{\bf I}-E\left[2{\bm{\beta}}{\bf X}^{\rm T}Yv({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})+{\bf a}(Y)\{Y-E(Y\mid{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}{\bf X}^{\rm T}\right]\right)

(S.6)

\displaystyle\times\left[E\{{\bf X}{\bf X}^{\rm T}v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}\right]^{-1},

where
{\bf a}(y)
satisfies (
S.5
).

Gathering the above derivations and results, we obtain
the summary description of the efficient influence function as

\displaystyle\bm{\phi}_{\rm eff}(y,{\bf x})={\bm{\beta}}v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x})-{\bm{\beta}}E\{v({\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf X})\}+{\bm{\beta}}y^{2}+{\bf M}{\bf x}y+{\bf a}(y)-E\{{\bm{\beta}}Y^{2}+{\bf M}{\bf x}Y+{\bf a}(Y)\mid{\bf x}\},

where
{\bf a}(y)
satisfies (
S.5
) and
{\bf M}
is given in (
S.6
).
Obviously, the variance of the efficient influence function,
i.e.
\hbox{var}\{\bm{\phi}_{\rm eff}(Y,{\bf X})\}
, is the efficiency bound in
estimating
{\bm{\xi}}
.

S.2
Derivation of the Efficiency Bound of Quantile Effect Estimation

Recall that for an arbitrary parametric submodel in
(
S.1
), we have already derived the corresponding score
functions

{\bf S}_{\bm{\alpha}}({\bf x})
,

{\bf S}_{\bm{\beta}}(y,{\bf x})
and
{\bf S}_{\bm{\gamma}}(y,{\bf x})
.
Now for notational brevity, let

\nu\equiv{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}{\bf x}
,

q(\nu)\equiv Q_{\tau}(Y\mid\nu)
,
\epsilon\equiv\tau-I\{Y<q(\nu)\}
,
and
\epsilon^{\prime}_{\nu}=-\delta\{q(\nu)-Y\}q^{\prime}(\nu)
.
Write
f(y,\nu)\equiv f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y,\nu)
.
Using the quantile definition,
we further have

\displaystyle E(\epsilon\mid\nu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle E(\epsilon Y\mid\nu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f\{q(\nu),\nu\}q^{\prime}(\nu),

\displaystyle\frac{E(\epsilon Y^{2}\mid\nu)}{f\{q(\nu),\nu\}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2q(\nu)q^{\prime}(\nu)+q^{\prime}(\nu)^{2}[\nu+c^{\prime}\{q(\nu)\}]+q^{\prime\prime}(\nu),

which can be verified based on

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E[I\{Y\leq q(\nu)\}\mid\nu],

\displaystyle q^{\prime}(\nu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{E\left([\tau-I\{Y<q(\nu)\}]Y\mid\nu\right)}{f\{q(\nu),\nu\}},

\displaystyle q^{\prime\prime}(\nu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{E\left([\tau-I\{Y<q(\nu)\}]Y^{2}\mid\nu\right)}{f\{q(\nu),\nu\}}-q(\nu)q^{\prime}(\nu)-q^{\prime}(\nu)[q(\nu)+q^{\prime}(\nu)\nu+q^{\prime}(\nu)c^{\prime}\{q(\nu)\}].

We also have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial q(\nu)}{\partial{\bm{\gamma}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{E\left\{\epsilon{\bf a}(Y)\mid\nu\right\}}{f\{q(\nu),\nu\}},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial q^{\prime}(v)}{\partial{\bm{\gamma}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{E\left\{\epsilon Y{\bf a}(Y)\mid\nu\right\}}{f\{q(\nu),\nu\}}-{\bf a}\{q(\nu)\}q^{\prime}(\nu)-\frac{\partial q(\nu)}{\partial{\bm{\gamma}}}\left[q(\nu)+q^{\prime}(\nu)\nu+q^{\prime}(\nu)c^{\prime}\{q(\nu)\}\right].

Note that

{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}={\bm{\beta}}E\{q^{\prime}(\nu)\}
.
We can verify that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}}{\partial{\bm{\alpha}}^{\rm T}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bm{\beta}}E\{q^{\prime}(\nu){\bf a}^{\rm T}({\bf X})\},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}}{\partial{\bm{\beta}}^{\rm T}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\{q^{\prime}(\nu)\}{\bf I}+{\bm{\beta}}E[r(Y,\nu){\bf X}^{\rm T}\{Y-E(Y\mid\nu)\}],

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}}{\partial{\bm{\gamma}}^{\rm T}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bm{\beta}}E\left(r(Y,\nu)[{\bf a}^{\rm T}(Y)-E\{{\bf a}^{\rm T}(Y)\mid{\bf X}\}]\right),

where

\displaystyle r(Y,\nu)\equiv\frac{\epsilon Y+\epsilon^{\prime}_{\nu}-\epsilon[q(\nu)+q^{\prime}(\nu)\nu+q^{\prime}(\nu)c^{\prime}\{q(\nu)\}]}{f\{q(\nu),\nu\}}.

(S.7)

Hence, a possible influence function is

\displaystyle\bm{\phi}(y,{\bf x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bm{\beta}}q^{\prime}(\nu)-{\bm{\beta}}E\{q^{\prime}(\nu)\}+{\bm{\beta}}[r(Y,\nu)-E\{r(Y,\nu)\mid\nu\}]

\displaystyle+E\{q^{\prime}(\nu)\}{\bf B}[{\bf b}(y,{\bf x})-E\{{\bf b}(Y,{\bf x})\mid{\bf x}\}],

where

\displaystyle{\bf B}\equiv\left(E[{\bf b}(Y,{\bf X}){\bf X}^{\rm T}\{Y-E(Y\mid\nu)\}]\right)^{-1},

(S.8)

and
{\bf b}(y,{\bf x})
is such that

\displaystyle E\{{\bf b}(y,{\bf X})\mid y\}=E[E\{{\bf b}(Y,{\bf X})\mid{\bf X}\}\mid y].

(S.9)

We can verify that

\partial{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}/\partial{\bm{\theta}}^{\rm T}=E(\bm{\phi}{\bf S}_{\bm{\theta}}^{\rm T})
.
Note that we have derived the tangent space
\cal T
in Section
2.1
.
Let
\bm{\rho}(y,{\bf x})\equiv E\{q^{\prime}(\nu)\}{\bf B}{\bf b}(y,{\bf x})+{\bm{\beta}}r(y,\nu)-{\bf a}(y)
,

v(\nu)\equiv E(Y^{2}\mid\nu)-\{E(Y\mid\nu)\}^{2}
, and

\displaystyle{\bf M}_{1}\equiv E\left[\bm{\rho}(Y,{\bf X}){\bf X}^{\rm T}\{Y-E(Y\mid\nu)\}\right]\left[E\{{\bf X}{\bf X}^{\rm T}v(\nu)\}\right]^{-1},

(S.10)

where
{\bf a}(y)
is such that

\displaystyle E[E\{{\bf a}(Y)\mid{\bf X}\}\mid y]-{\bf a}(y)=-{\bm{\beta}}E\{r(y,\nu)|y\}+{\bf M}_{1}E[{\bf X}\{y-E(Y|{\bf X})\}|y].

(S.11)

Then the efficient influence function is

\displaystyle\bm{\phi}_{\rm eff}(y,{\bf x})={\bm{\beta}}q^{\prime}(\nu)-{\bm{\beta}}E\{q^{\prime}(\nu)\}+{\bf M}_{1}{\bf x}y+{\bf a}(y)-E\{{\bf M}_{1}{\bf x}Y+{\bf a}(Y)|{\bf x}\},

(S.12)

where
{\bf a}(y)
satisfies (
S.11
),
{\bf M}_{1}
is given in (
S.10
),

{\bf b}(y,{\bf x})
satisfies (
S.9
) and
{\bf B}
is given in (
S.8
).
Note that once we have
{\bf a}(y)
, we can let

{\bf b}(y,{\bf x})={\bm{\beta}}r(y,\nu)-{\bf M}_{1}{\bf x}y-{\bf a}(y)
and it satisfies
(
S.9
). Using this specific
{\bf b}(y,{\bf x})
function,
we obtain that
{\bf M}_{1}
and
{\bf B}
need to satisfy

\displaystyle{\bf B}=\left(E[\{{\bm{\beta}}r(Y,\nu)-{\bf M}_{1}{\bf X}Y-{\bf a}(Y)\}{\bf X}^{\rm T}\{Y-E(Y\mid\nu)\}]\right)^{-1}

and

\displaystyle{\bf M}_{1}E\{{\bf X}{\bf X}^{\rm T}v(\nu)\}=E\{q^{\prime}(\nu)\}{\bf I}+{\bm{\beta}}E\{{\bf X}^{\rm T}q^{\prime\prime}(\nu)\}-E[{\bf a}(Y){\bf X}^{\rm T}\{Y-E(Y|{\bf X})\}].

This leads to

\displaystyle{\bf M}_{1}=(E\{q^{\prime}(\nu)\}{\bf I}+{\bm{\beta}}E\{{\bf X}^{\rm T}q^{\prime\prime}(\nu)\}-E[{\bf a}(Y){\bf X}^{\rm T}\{Y-E(Y|{\bf X})\}])[E\{{\bf X}{\bf X}^{\rm T}v(\nu)\}]^{-1},

(S.13)

where
{\bf a}(y)
satisfies (
S.11
).

Thus, in summary, the efficient influence function for estimating

{\bm{\eta}}_{\tau}
is given in (
S.12
),
where
{\bf a}(y)
satisfies (
S.11
),
r(Y,\nu)
is given in (
S.7
),
and
{\bf M}_{1}
is given in (
S.13
).

S.3
Lemmas

We first prove several lemmas that will be useful.

Lemma 1
.

Let
U\equiv\{{\bf u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\|{\bf u}\|_{2}=1\}
and
{\bf u}\in U
.
Under Conditions
(C2)
-
(C4)
,

\|{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\|_{2}\asymp h^{1/2}
,
\|{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\|_{1}=O(h^{1/2})

and
\|{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bf e}_{k}\|_{1}\asymp h
.

Proof.

We recall Lemma 1 in the supplement of
Jiang et al. 2018
,
which is a direct result from Theorem 5.4.2 on page 145 of
DeVore & Lorentz 1993
.
For each spline
\sum_{k=1}^{m}\gamma_{k}B_{k}(y)
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
,
there exists a constant
C_{r}>0
such that

\displaystyle C_{r}\lVert{\bm{\gamma}}^{\prime}\rVert_{p}\leq\left\lVert\sum_{k=1}^{m}\gamma_{k}B_{k}(\cdot)\right\rVert_{p}\leq\lVert{\bm{\gamma}}^{\prime}\rVert_{p},

where
{\bm{\gamma}}^{\prime}\equiv\{\gamma_{k}\{(t_{k}-t_{k-r})/r\}^{1/p},k=1,\dots,m\}^{\rm T}
.
This implies
\|{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\|_{p}\asymp\|{\bf u}^{\prime}\|_{p}
where

\displaystyle\|{\bf u}^{\prime}\|_{p}=\left[\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left\{u_{k}\left(\frac{t_{k}-t_{k-r}}{r}\right)^{1/p}\right\}^{p}\right]^{1/p}\asymp h^{1/p}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}u_{k}^{p}\right)^{1/p}=h^{1/p}\|{\bf u}\|_{p}

by Conditions
(C3)
and
(C4)
.
Hence we have
\|{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\|_{2}\asymp h^{1/2}
.
In addition, note that
\|{\bf u}\|_{1}=O(m^{1/2})\asymp O(h^{-1/2})
under Conditions
(C3)
and
(C4)
,
which leads to

\displaystyle\|{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\|_{1}\asymp h\times O(h^{-1/2})=O(h^{1/2}).

Since
\|{\bf e}_{k}\|_{1}=1
, we get
\|{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bf e}_{k}\|_{1}\asymp h
.
∎

Lemma 2
.

Let
U\equiv\{{\bf u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\|{\bf u}\|_{2}=1\}
and
{\bf u}\in U
.
Under Conditions
(C1)
-
(C4)
,

\displaystyle E[\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\}^{2}|{\bf x}]

\displaystyle\asymp

\displaystyle h,

\displaystyle E\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}|{\bf x}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(h^{1/2}),

\displaystyle E\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf e}_{k}|{\bf x}\}

\displaystyle\asymp

\displaystyle h,

\displaystyle\hbox{cov}\{Y,{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}|{\bf x}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(h^{1/2})

uniformly in
{\bf x}
. For fixed
{\bm{\beta}}
and
{\bm{\gamma}}
,
we have the same result under the density
f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}^{*}(y|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}})
.

Proof.

The result for
f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}^{*}(y|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}})
can be obtained similarly so we omit the proof.
We write
E[\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\}^{2}|{\bf x}]
as

\displaystyle E[\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\}^{2}|{\bf x}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\left\{{\bf B}(y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\right\}^{2}f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y|{\bf x})dy

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\{f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(\cdot|{\bf x})\}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

Note that under Condition
(C1)
,
there exist constants
0<c_{f}\leq C_{f}<\infty
such that

\displaystyle c_{f}\leq\inf_{y\in[0,1],{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y|{\bf x})\leq\sup_{y\in[0,1],{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y|{\bf x})\leq C_{f},

which implies

\displaystyle c_{f}\left\lVert{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\inf_{{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\{f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(\cdot|{\bf x})\}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\{f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(\cdot|{\bf x})\}^{1/2}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{f}\left\lVert{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

Then by Lemma
1
, we have
E[\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\}^{2}|{\bf x}]\asymp h
.
Further,
E\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}|{\bf x}\}
satisfies

\displaystyle E\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}|{\bf x}\}=\int_{0}^{1}{\bf B}(y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y|{\bf x})dy\leq C_{f}\left\lVert{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\right\rVert_{1}.

By Lemma
1
, the above implies
E\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}|{\bf x}\}=O(h^{1/2})
.
Similarly, we get
E\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf e}_{k}|{\bf x}\}\asymp h
.
Now,
\hbox{cov}\{Y,{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}|{\bf x}\}
satisfies

\displaystyle\hbox{cov}\{Y,{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}|{\bf x}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\{y-E(Y|{\bf x})\}{\bf B}(y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y|{\bf x})dy

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\|_{1}\sup_{y\in[0,1],{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|y-E(Y|{\bf x})|f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y|{\bf x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(h^{1/2}).

The last equality holds
because
\sup_{y\in[0,1],{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|y-E(Y|{\bf x})|f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y|{\bf x})
is bounded by Condition
(C1)

and
\|{\bf B}(\cdot)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\|_{1}=O(h^{1/2})
by Lemma
1
.
∎

Lemma 3
.

Let
U\equiv\{{\bf u}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\|{\bf u}\|_{2}=1\}
and
{\bf u}\in U
.
Under Conditions
(C1)
-
(C4)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf u}\in U}\hbox{var}\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}|{\bf x}\}\asymp h

uniformly in
{\bf x}
. For fixed
{\bm{\beta}}
and
{\bm{\gamma}}
,
we have
\sup_{{\bf u}\in U}\hbox{var}^{*}\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}}\}\asymp h
uniformly in
{\bf x}
.

Proof.

We omit the proof for
\hbox{var}^{*}\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}},{\bm{\gamma}}\}

because we can obtain the result by a similar way.
The variance
\hbox{var}\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}|{\bf x}\}
satisfies

\displaystyle\hbox{var}\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}|{\bf x}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E[\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}\}^{2}|{\bf x}]-[E\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}|{\bf x}\}]^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h-O(h)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(h)

uniformly in
{\bf x}
by Lemma
2
.
Similarly, we get
\hbox{var}\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf e}_{k}|{\bf x}\}\asymp h-h^{2}\asymp h
by Lemma
2
.
Then since
{\bf e}_{k}\in U
and
U
is compact, we get

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf u}\in U}\hbox{var}\{{\bf B}(Y)^{\rm T}{\bf u}|{\bf x}\}\asymp h

uniformly in
{\bf x}
under Condition
(C1)
.
∎

Lemma 4
.

Let
g_{1}(\cdot)
be a function such that
\|g_{1}(\cdot)\|_{1}<\infty
.
Under Conditions
(C1)
-
(C5)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in[0,1],{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}^{*}(y,{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}_{0},{\bm{\gamma}}_{0})-f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y|{\bf x})|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(h^{q}),

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|E^{*}\{g_{1}(Y)|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}_{0},{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}\}-E\{g_{1}(Y)|{\bf x}\}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(h^{q})\|g_{1}(\cdot)\|_{1}.

In addition, if
g_{2}(\cdot)
satisfies
\|g_{2}(\cdot)\|_{1}<\infty
and
\|g_{1}(\cdot)g_{2}(\cdot)\|_{1}<\infty
,

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|\hbox{cov}^{*}\{g_{1}(Y),g_{2}(Y)|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}_{0},{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}\}-\hbox{cov}\{g_{1}(Y),g_{2}(Y)|{\bf x}\}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(h^{q})\{\|g_{1}(\cdot)g_{2}(\cdot)\|_{1}+\|g_{1}(\cdot)\|_{1}\|g_{2}(\cdot)\|_{1}\}.

Proof.

Conditions
(C1)
-
(C5)
implies there exists a constant
0<C_{f}^{*}<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in[0,1],{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}^{*}(y,{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}_{0},{\bm{\gamma}}_{0})-f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y|{\bf x})|\leq C_{f}^{*}h^{q}.

This implies

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|E^{*}\{g_{1}(Y)|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}_{0},{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}\}-E\{g_{1}(Y)|{\bf x}\}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}\left|\int_{0}^{1}g_{1}(y)\{f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}^{*}(y,{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}_{0},{\bm{\gamma}}_{0})-f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y|{\bf x})\}dy\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|g_{1}(\cdot)\|_{1}\sup_{y\in[0,1],{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}^{*}(y,{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}_{0},{\bm{\gamma}}_{0})-f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y|{\bf x})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{f}^{*}h^{q}\|g_{1}(\cdot)\|_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(h^{q})\|g_{1}(\cdot)\|_{1}.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|E^{*}\{g_{1}(Y)g_{2}(Y)|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}_{0},{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}\}-E\{g_{1}(Y)g_{2}(Y)|{\bf x}\}|=O(h^{q})\|g_{1}(\cdot)g_{2}(\cdot)\|_{1}.

Now, note that under Condition
(C1)
, there exists a constant
0<C_{f}<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in[0,1],{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y|{\bf x})\leq C_{f}.

Then we get

\displaystyle|E^{*}\{g_{1}(Y)|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}_{0},{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}\}E^{*}\{g_{2}(Y)|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}_{0},{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}\}-E\{g_{1}(Y)|{\bf x}\}E\{g_{2}(Y)|{\bf x}\}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|O(h^{q})E\{g_{1}(Y)|{\bf x}\}\|g_{2}(\cdot)\|_{1}+O(h^{q})\|g_{1}(\cdot)\|_{1}E\{g_{2}(Y)|{\bf x}\}+O(h^{2q})\|g_{1}(\cdot)\|_{1}\|g_{2}(\cdot)\|_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle O(h^{q})\{2C_{f}+O(h^{q})\}\|g_{1}(\cdot)\|_{1}\|g_{2}(\cdot)\|_{1}

uniformly in
{\bf x}
by Condition
(C1)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|\hbox{cov}^{*}\{g_{1}(Y),g_{2}(Y)|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}_{0},{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}\}-\hbox{cov}\{g_{1}(Y),g_{2}(Y)|{\bf x}\}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|E^{*}\{g_{1}(Y)g_{2}(Y)|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}_{0},{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}\}-E\{g_{1}(Y)g_{2}(Y)|{\bf x}\}|

\displaystyle+\sup_{{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|E^{*}\{g_{1}(Y)|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}_{0},{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}\}E^{*}\{g_{2}(Y)|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}_{0},{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}\}-E\{g_{1}(Y)|{\bf x}\}E\{g_{2}(Y)|{\bf x}\}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(h^{q})\{\|g_{1}(\cdot)g_{2}(\cdot)\|_{1}+\|g_{1}(\cdot)\|_{1}\|g_{2}(\cdot)\|_{1}\}.

∎

Lemma 5
.

Let
g_{1}(\cdot)
be a function which satisfies
\|g_{1}(\cdot)\|_{1}<\infty
.
Let
{\bm{\beta}}^{*},{\bm{\gamma}}^{*}
satisfy
\|{\bm{\beta}}^{*}-{\bm{\beta}}_{0}\|_{2}=o_{p}(1)
and
\|{\bm{\gamma}}^{*}-{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}\|_{2}=o_{p}(1)
.
Under Conditions
(C1)
-
(C5)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in[0,1],{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}^{*}(y,{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}^{*},{\bm{\gamma}}^{*})-f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y|{\bf x})|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o_{p}(1),

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|E^{*}\{g_{1}(Y)|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}^{*},{\bm{\gamma}}^{*}\}-E\{g_{1}(Y)|{\bf x}\}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o_{p}(1)\|g_{1}(\cdot)\|_{1}.

In addition, if
g_{2}(\cdot)
satisfies
\|g_{2}(\cdot)\|_{1}<\infty
and

\|g_{1}(\cdot)g_{2}(\cdot)\|_{1}<\infty
, then

\displaystyle\sup_{{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|\hbox{cov}^{*}\{g_{1}(Y),g_{2}(Y)|{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}^{*},{\bm{\gamma}}^{*}\}-\hbox{cov}\{g_{1}(Y),g_{2}(Y)|{\bf x}\}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o_{p}(1)\{\|g_{1}(\cdot)g_{2}(\cdot)\|_{1}+\|g_{1}(\cdot)\|_{1}\|g_{2}(\cdot)\|_{1}\}.

Proof.

The result can be obtained similarly to Lemma
4

by noting that under Conditions
(C1)
-
(C5)
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in[0,1],{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}^{*}(y,{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}^{*},{\bm{\gamma}}^{*})-f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y|{\bf x})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in[0,1],{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}^{*}(y,{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}^{*},{\bm{\gamma}}^{*})-f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}^{*}(y,{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}_{0},{\bm{\gamma}}_{0})|

\displaystyle+\sup_{y\in[0,1],{\bf x}\in\mathcal{X}}|f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}^{*}(y,{\bf x},{\bm{\beta}}_{0},{\bm{\gamma}}_{0})-f_{Y\mid{\bf X}}(y|{\bf x})|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o_{p}(1).

∎

S.4
Proof of Proposition
1

First, we will show that
\|\widehat{\bm{\gamma}}({\bm{\beta}}_{0})-{\bm{\gamma}}_{0}\|_{2}=O_{p}\{(nh)^{-1/2}\}
.
Notice that the Hessian of the loglikelihood with respect to
{\bm{\gamma}}
